Well, I’m here because something’s been going on with the environmental modeling contract I’m working on. I do mathematical modeling, mostly, and I’ve led work like this before—though people act like I haven’t, which is sort of the point.

I’m pretty sure some of the people on that contract are tracking my revisions, watching what I change, and then planting errors in the datasets so it looks like I’m the one who can’t keep the work straight. My wife says it’s probably just version-control mistakes, and the junior analysts say the same thing, but they don’t see the pattern. But I haven’t really been tracking it as a mood thing.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the concerns about your work files, have you noticed any change in how you're managing other parts of your daily life—like relationships, self-care, or other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep the rest of things normal, but it’s gotten messy. My wife has been on me about basic routines—meals, getting moving, just keeping things in order. Four to six months… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve always been careful with revisions. In modeling work you have to be, because one bad change can sit there unnoticed and then suddenly the whole thing is off—people think it’s just a formatting issue, but it’s not.

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It hasn’t really gone away, no. I mean, some hours I’m not actively thinking about it—if I’m eating dinner or my wife is talking about something with the house—but then I look back at the files and it’s right there again.

People keep saying “version control,” like that explains everything, but I’ve worked with datasets long enough to know when something is being changed after the fact. The junior analysts act like it’s just confusion, and my wife wants me to step away from it, but… I’m pretty sure they’ve been tracking my revisions and putting errors in to make it look like I can’t lead the contract. So, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch where I stopped believing it.

Back when I was doing finite element stuff, you could almost tell from the fan pitch when a run was going bad, which sounds strange but it’s true.\n\nBut, uh… no, I haven’t really noticed things like seeing something other people don’t see. Not in that sense. The issue is more the pattern in the work files.\n\nDoctor: Over these past two months, have you also noticed changes in your mood—like feeling unusually depressed, hopeless, or on the other end, unusually elevated, energetic, or needing much less sleep?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it in those terms. I mean, anybody would get discouraged when people keep acting like your work is sloppy, especially when you’ve spent years learning how to catch numerical problems before they propagate. There was this one project years ago where a tiny rounding issue threw off a whole model, and everyone blamed the inputs first—\n\nBut mood, like depressed or elevated… I don’t know. I’ve been stressed, I guess. Annoyed. Tired sometimes because I’m thinking about the files and what they’re doing with them. But I haven’t really been tracking it as a mood thing.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the concerns about your work files, have you noticed any change in how you're managing other parts of your daily life—like relationships, self-care, or other responsibilities?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I’ve tried to keep the rest of things normal, but it’s gotten messy. At work I’m behind on the modeling deliverables—far enough behind that the firm started reassigning parts of the contract to other people. And I’m supposed to be supervising the junior analysts, helping them catch mistakes, but lately I’m not really useful to them.\n\nAt home, I mostly keep to myself. My wife has been on me about basic routines—meals, getting moving, just keeping things in order. It’s not that I’m trying to let things slide, it’s just… once you’re watching the files and trying to reconstruct what happened, hours disappear.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months when things became clear to you, did you notice any earlier signs—even subtle ones, like feeling unusually suspicious of colleagues, or acting or thinking differently than your usual self—going back further, say four to six months ago?\n\nPatient: Four to six months… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve always been careful with revisions. In modeling work you have to be, because one bad change can sit there unnoticed and then suddenly the whole thing is off—people think it’s just a formatting issue, but it’s not.\n\nBut the part where I was really sure people on the project were tracking my revisions and putting errors into the files… that’s more like the last two months. Before that, maybe I was irritated or watching things more closely, but it wasn’t clear to me the way it is now.\n\nDoctor: Over these past two months, has this belief that your colleagues are tracking your revisions and planting errors been present continuously the whole time, or have there been any stretches—even a few days—where that belief went away or felt less certain?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t really gone away, no. I mean, some hours I’m not actively thinking about it—if I’m eating dinner or my wife is talking about something with the house—but then I look back at the files and it’s right there again.\n\nPeople keep saying “version control,” like that explains everything, but I’ve worked with datasets long enough to know when something is being changed after the fact. The junior analysts act like it’s just confusion, and my wife wants me to step away from it, but… I’m pretty sure they’ve been tracking my revisions and putting errors in to make it look like I can’t lead the contract. So, no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a stretch where I stopped believing it.\n\nDoctor: \n\nPatient: Yeah, it’s been about two months that I’ve been sure about it. I mean, before that I was checking things more carefully, but the part where it clicked—where it was obvious to me they were tracking my revisions and planting errors—that’s been these last couple months.\n\nIt’s hard to put an exact day on it, because with project files everything kind of blurs together. You open one spreadsheet, then another, then you’re comparing timestamps, and suddenly it’s midnight. But roughly… yeah, about two months.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

It was earlier than the part where I was sure about the files. The whole… decline, I guess you’d call it, started after they put me in charge of that larger environmental modeling contract.

At work I’m behind on the modeling deliverables—far enough behind that the firm started reassigning parts of the contract to other people. And I’m supposed to be supervising the junior analysts, helping them catch mistakes, but lately I’m not really useful to them.\n\nAt home, I mostly keep to myself. My wife has been on me about basic routines—meals, getting moving, just keeping things in order. It’s not that I’m trying to let things slide, it’s just… once you’re watching the files and trying to reconstruct what happened, hours disappear.\n\nDoctor: Before these past two months when things became clear to you, did you notice any earlier signs—even subtle ones, like feeling unusually suspicious of colleagues, or acting or thinking differently than your usual self—going back further, say four to six months ago?\n\nPatient: Four to six months… I don’t know. I mean, I’ve always been careful with revisions. In modeling work you have to be, because one bad change can sit there unnoticed and then suddenly the whole thing is off—people think it’s just a formatting issue, but it’s not.\n\nBut the part where I was really sure people on the project were tracking my revisions and putting errors into the files… that’s more like the last two months. Before that, maybe I was irritated or watching things more closely, but it wasn’t clear to me the way it is now.\n\nDoctor: Over these past two months, has this belief that your colleagues are tracking your revisions and planting errors been present continuously the whole time, or have there been any stretches—even a few days—where that belief went away or felt less certain?\n\nPatient: It hasn’t really gone away, no. I’d have to look at the exact dates if you mean every supplement bottle in the cabinet. My wife buys vitamins sometimes and they end up in a drawer. I’m not sure what counts there. Not in those words exactly. More like I’m sitting there but not really there.\n\nAnd yeah, I suppose I have pulled back. I can sit for hours at my desk, or out in the garage, and the telescope is right there still covered. I used to go to those astronomy nights, and hiking too, and I liked mentoring the younger analysts—well, some of them, when they actually wanted to learn and not just click around in dashboards like the software is doing the thinking for them.\n\nBut lately I just don’t feel much pull to start any of it. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).
